Option A: Extract kernel + initramfs and boot with `-kernel`, `-initrd`, `-append "alpine_dev=cdrom modloop=cdrom console=ttyS0 quiet"`. This is clean and deterministic.

Option B: Use QEMU's `-boot` with menu and send keystroke.

Let's try Option A. Need to extract.

Can we install pycdlib quickly via pip? Let's try.

Actually simpler: We might not even need to extract. QEMU's cdrom can be passed as a drive, and we can specify `-kernel /path/on/host`... but kernel is inside ISO, not on host. So need extraction.

Alternatively, use `xorriso`/`7z` install via apt? May not have network.

xorriso syntax is a bit different. The `-extract` command extracts to current working dir or specified. Actually `xorriso -osirrox on -indev isofile -extract /BOOT/SYSLINUX /tmp/outdir`. The second positional argument is the output directoryPrefix. Let me run: `xorriso -osirrox on -indev /app/alpine.iso -extract /BOOT/SYSLINUX /tmp/isox`. Hmm.

Actually the syntax is: `xorriso -osirrox on -indev ISO -extract SRC DEST` where DEST is a target path on the filesystem. Let me just use `-extract` with cd to /tmp/isox.

The initramfs doesn't have inittab. The initramfs's init script will mount the modloop and setup root, then hand over to the "real" root which comes from modloop (the APKs). The actual root filesystem (with inittab, getty, etc.) is in the mounted modloop: `/boot/modloop-lts` (180MB squashfs). That's where the real Alpine userland lives)Skip. On Alpine ISO, after boot the kernel switches to a "root" from the squashfs modloop with a writable overlay.

So serial login depends on the real root's inittab (from modloop). Let me extract modloop-lts and inspect its etc/inittab. It's a squashfs (180MB). Let me check `-x` with the squashfs file. Interesting — modloop only has `modules`. So the initramfs userland is the entire userland? Wait, but the initramfs has bin/busybox, libs, sbin, etc. So the root filesystem IS extracted from initramfs + APKs. Actually in Alpine's boot process, the initramfs includes the actual root with busybox; the `.apk` files in /APKS/X86_64 are installed to / (root) during boot using `apk` from the initramfs? Let me recall: Alpine ISO boot: kernel + initramfs; init mounts the squashfs modloop which contains modules and the APK repo; it then runs `apk --root $target add ...`? Hmm.

Actually the Alpine "initramfs" here is quite large (20MB) and has busybox, userspace libs. But the real userland with inittab, agetty, etc. comes from apk packages in /APKS/X86_64, which are installed at boot into a tmpfs "root" (also known as `alpine_dev`). The boot process:
1. initramfs init script loads modloop (squashfs with modules and OVECKED apks?).
2. It sets up a tmpfs, copies the base system (busybox etc.) and installs packages from modloop/APKS.

So the login prompt: does Alpine ISO show a login prompt at all? Yes — after booting the ISO, you get `localhost login:` on the console. There is a getty process launched on tty consoles. The getty comes from the `agetty` apk(6.6.4) in APKS. The inittab with getty lines comes from `openrc`/`busybox-initscripts`? Actually Alpine's default /etc/inittab is owned by `busybox-initscripts` package, which is present among APKS.

So we need to boot with parameters including `console=ttyS0` to set up serial. But the APKS install happens during boot; during init, the inittab is configured based on console boot param likely.

Let me examine the init script (initramfs /init) to understand serial console handling.

[tool-054] exec_command -> splashfile=/.splash.ctrl
[tool-054] exec_command -> repofile="$ROOT"/tmp/repositories
[tool-054] exec_command -> 
[tool-054] exec_command -> # some helpers
[tool-054] exec_command -> ebegin() {
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	last_emsg="$*"
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	echo "$last_emsg..." > "$ROOT"/dev/kmsg
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	[ "$KOPT_quiet" = yes ] && return 0
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	echo -n " * $last_emsg: "
[tool-054] exec_command -> }
[tool-054] exec_command -> eend() {
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	local msg
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	if [ "$1" = 0 ] || [ $# -lt 1 ] ; then
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		echo "$last_emsg: ok." > "$ROOT"/dev/kmsg
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		[ "$KOPT_quiet" = yes ] && return 0
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		echo "ok."
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	else
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		shift
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		echo "$last_emsg: failed. $*" > "$ROOT"/dev/kmsg
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		if [ "$KOPT_quiet" = "yes" ]; then
[tool-054] exec_command -> 			echo -n "$last_emsg "
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		fi
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		echo "failed. $*"
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		echo "initramfs emergency recovery shell launched. Type 'exit' to continue boot"

[tool-054] exec_command -> unpack_apkovl() {
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	local ovl="$1"
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	local dest="$2"
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	local suffix=${ovl##*.}
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	local i
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	ovlfiles=/tmp/ovlfiles
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	if [ "$suffix" = "gz" ]; then
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		tar -C "$dest" -zxvf "$ovl" > $ovlfiles
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		return $?
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	fi
[tool-054] exec_command -> 
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	# we need openssl. let apk handle deps
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	apk add --quiet --initdb --repositories-file $repofile openssl || return 1
[tool-054] exec_command -> 
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	if ! openssl list -1 -cipher-commands | grep "^$suffix$" > /dev/null; then
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		errstr="Cipher $suffix is not supported"
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		return 1
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	fi
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	local count=0
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	# beep
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	echo -e "\007"
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	while [ $count -lt 3 ]; do
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		openssl enc -d -$suffix -in "$ovl" | tar --numeric-owner \
[tool-054] exec_command -> 			-C "$dest" -zxv >$ovlfiles 2>/dev/null && return 0
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		count=$(( $count + 1 ))
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	done
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	ovlfiles=
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	return 1
[tool-054] exec_command -> }

[tool-054] exec_command -> # find mount dir and mount opts for given device in an fstab
[tool-054] exec_command -> get_fstab_mount_info() {
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	local search_dev="$1"
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	local fstab="$2"
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	local mntopts=
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	case "$search_dev" in
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	UUID*|LABEL*) search_dev=$(findfs "$search_dev");;
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	esac
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	[ -r "$fstab" ] || return 1
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	local search_maj_min=$(stat -L -c '%t,%T' $search_dev)
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	while read dev mnt fs mntopts chk; do
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		case "$dev" in
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		UUID*|LABEL*) dev=$(findfs "$dev");;
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		esac
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		if [ -b "$dev" ] || [ -n "$ROOT" ]; then
[tool-054] exec_command -> 			local maj_min=$(stat -L -c '%t,%T' $dev)
[tool-054] exec_command -> 			if [ "$maj_min" = "$search_maj_min" ]; then
[tool-054] exec_command -> 				echo "$mnt $mntopts"
[tool-054] exec_command -> 				return
[tool-054] exec_command -> 			fi
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		fi
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	done < $fstab
[tool-054] exec_command -> }

[tool-054] exec_command -> detect_serial_consoles() {

[tool-054] exec_command -> 		return
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	fi

[tool-054] exec_command -> 			echo ${i##*/}
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		fi
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	done

[tool-054] exec_command -> setup_inittab_console() {
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	term=vt100
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	# Inquire the kernel for list of console= devices
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	consoles="$(for c in console $KOPT_consoles $(detect_serial_consoles); do list_console_devices $c; done)"
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	for tty in $consoles; do
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		# ignore tty devices that gives I/O error
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		if ! stty -g -F /dev/$tty >/dev/null 2>/dev/null; then
[tool-054] exec_command -> 			continue
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		fi
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		# do nothing if inittab already have the tty set up
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		if ! grep -q "^$tty:" $sysroot/etc/inittab 2>/dev/null; then
[tool-054] exec_command -> 			echo "# enable login on alternative console" \
[tool-054] exec_command -> 				>> $sysroot/etc/inittab
[tool-054] exec_command -> 			# Baudrate of 0 keeps settings from kernel
[tool-054] exec_command -> 			echo "$tty::respawn:/sbin/getty -L 0 $tty $term" \
[tool-054] exec_command -> 				>> $sysroot/etc/inittab
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		fi
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		if [ -e "$sysroot"/etc/securetty ] && ! grep -q -w "$tty" "$sysroot"/etc/securetty; then
[tool-054] exec_command -> 			echo "$tty" >> "$sysroot"/etc/securetty
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		fi
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	done
[tool-054] exec_command -> }

[tool-054] exec_command -> # determine the default interface to use if ip=dhcp is set

[tool-054] exec_command -> 		[ -n "$dev" ] && echo "${dev##*/}" && return
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	fi
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	for x in "$ROOT"/sys/class/net/eth*; do
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		if grep -iq up $x/operstate 2>/dev/null;then
[tool-054] exec_command -> 			[ -e "$x" ] && echo ${x##*/} && return
[tool-054] exec_command -> 		fi
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	done
[tool-054] exec_command -> 	[ -e "$x" ] && echo ${x##*/} && return
[tool-054] exec_command -> }

[penguin] output cap clamped hard: max_tokens 256000 -> 93419 (context_window 128000, estimated input 33557)
Excellent! Key finding: Alpine's initramfs `init` script has `setup_inittab_console()` which:
- Determines consoles: for `console` (the boot param), `$KOPT_consoles`, and autodetected serial consoles.
- For each tty, if not already in inittab, appends `$tty::respawn:/sbin/getty -L 0 $tty $term` to inittab.
- Also adds to securetty.

So if we boot with kernel cmdline `console=ttyS0`, the `console` keyword will resolve (via /sys/class/tty/console/active) to `ttyS0`, and an inittab `ttyS0::respawn:/sbin/getty -L 0 ttyS0 vt100` will be added→ login prompt on serial! Also kernel messages go to ttyS0.

So the approach:
- Boot Alpine ISO with kernel cmdline including `console=ttyS0` (and possibly `modules=loop,squashfs,sd-mod,usb-storage`).
- Use `-kernel` + `-initrd` + `-append` from extracted vmlinuz/initrd, OR pass console to the ISO boot directly (which we can't easily because syslinux cfg is fixed).

Actually wait, we can boot the CD-ROM directly with syslinux but passing extra kernel args via isolinux's APPEND? Not from outside. So the cleanest is `-kernel /tmp/vmlinuz-lts -initrd ... -append "..."` with `-cdrom /app/alpine.iso` or `-drive` presenting the ISO as the alpine_dev source so it can find APKS.

Alpine ISO boot expects the ISO to be accessible: `alpine_dev=cdrom` normally. With our own kernel cmdline, we should specify `alpine_dev=cdrom` (or let init auto-detect). The initramfs also finds modloop on the ISO. Actually the boot normally passes `modules=loop,squashfs,sd-mod,usb-storage quiet` and `alpine_dev=cdrom`? The syslinux.cfg we saw has `APPEND modules=... quiet` (no alpine_dev). The init script auto-detects the alpine device? Let me check: init script has `KOPT_alpine_dev` default likely "cdrom". Let me search the init script for `alpine_dev` and `modloop`.

**Important**: The kernel cmdline `console=ttyS0` gets stored in `KOPT_consoles`, `switch_root_opts="-c /dev/ttyS0"`, and `setup_inittab_console` adds getty for devices from `console $KOPT_consoles ...`.

So if we boot with `console=ttyS0`:
1. Kernel console output → ttyS0 (serial).
2. `list_console_devices ttyS0` yields ttyS0 (since /sys/class/tty/ttyS0/active doesn't exist → echo ttyS0).
3. inittab gets `ttyS0::respawn:/sbin/getty -L 0 ttyS0 vt100` → login prompt on the serial console.

This is exactly what we need.

So when no `root=` is given, it does `nlplug-findfs ... $repoopts -a apkovls` to "locate boot media and mount it" — this mounts the boot media (the ISO) and modloop from it. This auto-detects the Alpine ISO/CD-ROM. So booting the ISO with our own kernel works as long as the ISO is attached as a cdrom.

The persistent session model: When we call exec_command, it runs a command in a persistent shell/session. If the command itself runs a long-running foreground process (like qemu), the session shell remains in the foreground running that process. The process_id is returned)Skip; we can keep sending input to it or check status. The session stays alive as long as... well, until its main command finishes or is killed.

But the tool returns control to us after yield_time_ms even if the command is still running. Then the session is in the background "running". We can reconnect via input_command to send chars.

The long-running foreground `sleep 61` (pid 1418) is alive in its session, even after the exec_command call returned (2s yield). So sessions persist as long as they keep running in the foreground.

So the model: exec_command spawns a shell that runs a command; if the command keeps running, the session persists and we can interact via input_command or check on it with other exec_commands. When the main command exits, the session ends.

So our approach: Launch qemu as a foreground command in an exec_command session, with yield_time_ms small enough to return quickly. qemu keeps running as the session's main process. It will keep running after we return the final answer. "Block until it's ready" — we poll via separate exec_command calls (e.g., connect to the telnet port and check for login prompt).

But careful: The exec_command session that runs qemu: the session's shell PID may be the process we keep. When we need to stop qemu later, we could use input_command... but the user said leave it running. Fine.

Also note that the prior attempt with `(qemu & ...)` got killed because that command's shell exited (its session ended). Right.

[tool-ac1] exec_command <- Connect via telnet and capture console output ($ cat > /tmp/telnet_capture.exp <<'EOF' #!/usr/bin/expect -f set timeout 15 spawn telnet 127.0.0.1 6665 log_user 1 set out [open /tmp/telnet_capture.out w] while {1} { set got [expect { -re {login:} { puts $out "LOGIN_PROMPT_SEEN"; break } timeout { puts $out "TIMEOUT"; break } eof { puts $out "EOF"; break } }] } close $out send_user "\n(capture finished)\n" EOF expect /tmp/telnet_capture.exp > /tmp/expect_console.log 2>&1 & EXPECTPID=$!; sleep 18; kill $EXPECTPID 2>/dev/null; wait $EXPECTPID 2>/dev/null; echo "--- capture file ---"; cat /tmp/telnet_capture.out 2>/dev/null; echo "--- expect console log (last 60 lines) ---"; tail -60 /tmp/expect_console.log)

But one gotcha: connecting to telnet port with a raw python socket — we saw earlier that the raw socket receives telnet negotiation bytes at connect time then data once guest sends. Sending nothing is fine; we just read. The "login:" string will appear without us sending anything. Earlier with raw python we connected to 6668 (raw TCP, guest panicked → 0 bytes). With telnet, first connect handshake: server sends IAC negotiation, then when guest writes, data flows. A raw socket (not doing telnet) will still receive all bytes (including IAC prefix), and the "login:" text will be present in the stream. But there's a catch: because QEMU's telnet backend may **wait for the client's response to negotiation before forwarding serial output**? In our earlier successful post-login interaction we connected with GNU telnet (which negotiated) and received data. I'll build a poller in expect: spawn telnet, loop expecting either "login:" (success) or timeout (keep waiting) up to a cap. Actually `expect` handles the telnet negotiation automatically. Each `expect` with a timeout of, say, 20s; total running in a loop.

Let me write a standalone expect poller:

```expect
#!/usr/bin/expect -f
set timeout 20
set attempts 0
spawn telnet 127.0.0.1 6665
while {1} {
  incr attempts
  expect {
    -re {login: *} { send_user "READY after $attempts\n"; exit 0 }
    timeout { send_user "attempt $attempts: no prompt yet ($::...)\n"; }
    eof { exit 1 }
  }
  # reconnect if connection died?
}
```

Hmm, if the connection persists but guest hadn't yet attached getty, a single expect with timeout would keep waiting. Whether the connection persists across a mid-boot period: QEMU telnet server keeps the connection; when guest hasn't output anything yet, the recv just yields nothing → expect timeout → loop continues to next expect on the same spawn. That's fine: the connection stays open.

But there is a catch: on timeout, `expect` leaves the spawn's input buffer as-is; fine.

I can also cap total attempts (e.g., 40 × 20s = 800s ≈ 13 min).

Let me create /tmp/poll.exp:

```
#!/usr/bin/expect -f
set log [open "/tmp/alpine_ready.log" w]
set timeout 20
spawn telnet 127.0.0.1 6665
set attempts 0
while 1 {
    incr attempts
    expect {
        -re {localhost login:} {
            puts $log "READY after attempts=$attempts time=[clock format [clock seconds]]"
            close $log
            send_user "READY\n"
            exit 0
        }
        timeout {
            puts $log "attempt $attempts timeout [clock seconds]"
            flush $log
        }
        eof {
            puts $log "EOF at attempt $attempts"
            close $log
            exit 1
        }
    }
}
```

Concern: The telnet `spawn` may connect before the port is up? QEMU begins listening immediately (nowait). Fine.

Also, we need `-re {localhost login:}`? The exact prompt in our earlier capture was `localhost login: root`. Maybe it's `localhost login:` — with hostname "localhost". But hostname might differ? The ISO default hostname is `localhost`. We saw exactly `localhost login:`. To be robust, match `login:`.

Typically getty stays attached to the tty line forever (doesn't exit on disconnect, unless the line drops DCD). With a real serial port, when the client (modem) drops, getty sees no carrier and may respawn... but over telnet, the telnet server maintains the connection, so DCD may never drop. Since QEMU's telnet chardev keeps the socket, getty never sees a hangup when client disconnects? Actually when the client disconnects, does QEMU's telnet backend close the guest's serial? The chardev in server mode accepts connections; when the client closes, data just stops; getty may not see hangup. So getty keeps waiting for username from an empty line.

Thus the user will likely connect and see a blank console until they press Enter. That's the typical experience for Alpine serial console in QEMU with telnet: connect and press Enter to get the login.
